Problem sheet 6 · Nonlinear frequencies and broken symmetry

Identify nonlinear stiffness and track equilibria as a rotating constraint changes stability.

Fit a weak cubic spring

A mass of 0.20 kg has a natural frequency of 10.00 Hz at negligible amplitude. At 10.0 mm amplitude it oscillates at 10.03 Hz. Assume a restoring force \(-kx-\beta x^3\). Estimate \(k\) and \(\beta\), predict the shift at 5.0 mm and test the perturbation assumption.

Show the stiffness estimates

The linear coefficient is \(k=m(2\pi f_0)^2=790\) N/m. Retaining the fundamental of \(\cos^3\omega t\) gives \(\Delta f/f_0\simeq3\beta A^2/(8k)\). Hence \(\beta=8k(0.0030)/(3\times10^{-4})=6.32\times10^4\) N/m³. The positive sign describes hardening. At half amplitude the shift falls by four, to \(0.0075\) Hz. The maximum cubic-to-linear force ratio at 10 mm is \(\beta A^2/k=0.0080\), compatible with a first-order estimate.

Which equilibria can hold a bead?

A bead slides without friction on a vertical hoop of radius 0.400 m rotating about its vertical diameter. Find the critical angular speed and stable positions at 6.00 rad/s. Determine the small oscillation frequency about either tilted position and compare with operation at 4.00 rad/s.

Show the stability calculation

With angle measured from the bottom the effective potential per unit mass is \(-gR\cos\theta-\Omega^2R^2\sin^2\theta/2\). Its derivative is \(R\sin\theta(g-\Omega^2R\cos\theta)\). The critical speed is \(\sqrt{g/R}=4.95\) rad/s. At 6.00 rad/s the tilted minima have \(\cos\theta=9.81/(36\times0.4)=0.6813\), hence \(\theta=\pm47.1^\circ\). Curvature divided by \(R^2\) gives \(\omega^2=\Omega^2\sin^2\theta\), or \(\omega=4.39\) rad/s. At 4.00 rad/s only the bottom is stable and \(\omega=\sqrt{g/R-\Omega^2}=2.92\) rad/s.

Tilt a symmetric double well

For dimensionless displacement \(x\), a potential is \(V/E_0=x^4/4-ax^2/2-hx\) with \(a=0.12\). Find equilibria and barrier height at \(h=0\). Determine the magnitude of bias where one metastable well disappears and explain why the transition can depend on sweep history.

Show the barrier and the loss of stability

At zero bias stationary points solve \(x(x^2-a)=0\). Curvature \(3x^2-a\) gives minima at \(\pm\sqrt{a}=\pm0.346\) and a maximum at zero. The energy barrier is \(a^2E_0/4=0.00360E_0\). A minimum and adjacent maximum merge when \(3x^2=a\) and \(h=x^3-ax\). Thus \(|h|=2(a/3)^{3/2}=0.0160\). A weakly damped or cold system initially captured in the unfavoured well can remain there as the bias crosses zero. In a slowly driven, damped experiment without appreciable fluctuations it switches near the disappearance of that minimum; noise can trigger earlier switching.

Reconstruct a binary orbit from a spectrum

Two spectral lines trace a circular binary with a period of 20.0 days. Their radial-velocity semi-amplitudes are 30.0 and 60.0 km/s. Take the orbit to be edge-on. Find the stellar separation, both masses and the orbital energy. How would the mass estimate change at an inclination of 60°? Use G = 6.6743 × 10⁻¹¹ in SI units and one solar mass = 1.9885 × 10³⁰ kg.

Worked solution

The two stars orbit their common centre of mass with the same angular frequency. Their speeds add to give a relative circular speed of 90,000 m/s. Over 1,728,000 s the relative separation vector describes a circle of circumference 2πa, hence a = 2.475 × 10¹⁰ m. The speed ratio gives the inverse mass ratio. The star moving at 30 km/s is twice as massive as the other star.

The relative acceleration is v²/a and the two-body attraction gives G(M₁+M₂)/a². Equating the expressions gives 3.00 × 10³⁰ kg in total, about 1.51 solar masses. The individual masses are approximately 1.01 and 0.50 solar masses. The reduced mass is two ninths of the total. Its kinetic energy is ½μv², and the total orbital energy is its negative, approximately −2.70 × 10³⁹ J.

If the inclination falls to 60°, the observed speeds are projections of the larger orbital speeds. The separation scales as 1/sin i, and the inferred mass as 1/sin³ i. The total rises by a factor of 1.54. Spectroscopy alone fixes the mass ratio and a projected mass. An independent inclination from an eclipse or astrometric orbit breaks the degeneracy.

Chapter 13 · Changing the coordinates of a moving trap

A translating trap looks stationary in its own coordinates. Derive the Hamiltonian measured by that observer, including the term that accounts for motion of the coordinate system.

What does a change of coordinates have to preserve?

A counter moving along a rail reports the bead position relative to the counter. Its reading alone does not transform the bead momentum or tell us how energy changes in the moving frame. Let the laboratory Hamiltonian be \(H(q,p,t)\). The phase-space action integrates \(p\dot q-H\) between specified configurations. Adding a total time derivative changes that integral only at the ends. It therefore provides a way to construct new coordinates in which the same interior trajectories make the action stationary.

Demand that the old and new action forms differ by an exact differential. A convenient function uses the old position and the new momentum as arguments. Subtract \(QP\) from it to convert the independent end variables. The product rule gives

\[p\,dq-H\,dt=P\,dQ-K\,dt+d(F_2-QP)=dF_2-Q\,dP-K\,dt .\]

Now compare the coefficients of the independent differentials separately. The new energy has an extra term whenever the coordinate map depends explicitly on time.

\[p=\partial_q F_2,\]
\[Q=\partial_P F_2,\]
\[K=H+\partial_t F_2 .\]

For a simple test take \(F_2=\lambda qP\) with constant positive \(\lambda\). Then \(Q=\lambda q\) and \(P=p/\lambda\). A position interval stretches while the conjugate momentum interval shrinks by the reciprocal factor. The product \(dq\,dp\) stays fixed. This checks the derivative rules without using a particular force law.

Move the origin and then move the momentum

Take the trap centre to be \(a(t)\) and the laboratory potential to be \(k(q-a)^2/2\). Choosing \(F_2=(q-a)P\) gives \(Q=q-a,\ p=P\). Thus the coordinate is relative position, but its conjugate momentum still equals the laboratory momentum. Hamilton's velocity equation reads \(\dot Q=P/m-\dot a\), exactly the velocity seen by the moving counter.

To obtain a momentum equal to \(m\dot Q\), choose \(F_2=(q-a)(P+m\dot a)+g(t)\). This produces \(p=P+m\dot a\). Substitute it into the kinetic energy and the explicit time derivative of the generator. The cross terms \(\dot a P\) cancel. The remaining terms depending on time alone can be removed by choosing \(\dot g=m\dot a^2/2\). The result is

\[K=\frac{P^2}{2m}+\frac{kQ^2}{2}+m\ddot a\,Q,\]
\[m\ddot Q=-kQ-m\ddot a .\]

The apparent force comes from the acceleration of the reference point. A constant translation speed drops out of this final equation. A time-dependent acceleration can do work on the relative motion, so the bead's relative mechanical energy need not be conserved.

Predict a displaced equilibrium

Use a 0.50 kg bead and a spring with \(k=8.0\) N/m. Accelerate the support uniformly at \(1.6\) m/s² in the positive direction. A stationary bead in the accelerating frame has \(Q_*=-m\ddot a/k=-0.100\) m. Relative oscillations about this new centre have the original angular frequency \(4.0\) rad/s. The curvature of the potential sets the frequency and the added linear term changes its minimum.

If the bead is initially at \(Q=0\) with zero relative velocity when acceleration starts, its subsequent displacement is \(Q=Q_*[1-\cos(\omega t)]\). At \(t=\pi/\omega=0.7854\) s it reaches \(-0.200\) m, twice the static shift. It then returns. An overdamped measuring device would instead approach the static reading without that overshoot. Damping has been omitted here.

Set the support acceleration

Predict the sign of the first turning point when the acceleration reverses. Change the acceleration, then change the spring stiffness. Track separately the position of the equilibrium and the time between successive extrema.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on reconstructing the motion

A bead released from the old equilibrium reaches its first relative extremum at −0.060 m after 0.50 s. Find its angular frequency and the support acceleration.

Work through the solution

The first extremum occurs after half a cycle, giving \(\omega=\pi/0.50=6.283\) rad/s. The equilibrium shift is half the extremum, \(Q_*=-0.030\) m. The equation for that shift gives \(\ddot a=-\omega^2Q_*=1.18\) m/s². Both quantities follow from the trajectory without a separate mass measurement.

Check the momentum transformation directly by measuring kinetic energy. In a frame attached to a support moving at constant \(0.30\,\mathrm{m/s}\), a \(0.50\) kg bead has \(P=0.20\,\mathrm{kg\,m/s}\) along the support velocity. What laboratory momentum and kinetic energy should an observer assign?

Work through the solution

The moving-frame kinetic momentum is \(m(\dot q-\dot a)\). Thus \(p=P+m\dot a=0.35\,\mathrm{kg\,m/s}\), the relative kinetic energy is \(P^2/(2m)=0.0400\,\mathrm J\) and the laboratory kinetic energy is \(p^2/(2m)=0.1225\,\mathrm J\). Expanding the square gives the missing cross term \(P\dot a=0.0600\,\mathrm J\) and offset \(m\dot a^2/2=0.0225\,\mathrm J\).

A support acceleration varies with time. How could you reconstruct it from a measured relative trajectory, and which measurements become unreliable first in the presence of position noise?

Work through the solution

Rearranging the equation gives \(\ddot a=-\ddot Q-kQ/m\). A static deflection measures only constant acceleration after transients are controlled. In a time-dependent experiment a second time derivative is unavoidable for this inversion. It amplifies short-time position noise, so fitted trajectories or independent acceleration data can test the reconstruction.

Further reading · Fließbach, Mechanik, Hamiltonformalismus · Tong, Classical Dynamics, canonical transformations.

Chapter 14 · Recover a trajectory from a family of actions

In Hamilton–Jacobi theory a partial differential equation for one function replaces several trajectory equations. A falling bead makes the different roles of position, energy and arrival time explicit.

Use the end of the path as a variable

Let a bead move between a fixed initial event and an adjustable final event. Denote by \(S(q,t)\) the action evaluated on the actual trajectory ending at \((q,t)\). For a small change in the endpoint, the interior variation cancels by the Euler–Lagrange equation. If time stays fixed the boundary term is \(p\,\delta q\). If time changes, part of the endpoint displacement comes from continuing along the existing path by \(\dot q\,\delta t\). The remaining independent displacement is \(\delta q-\dot q\,\delta t\). Thus

\[\delta S=p(\delta q-\dot q\,\delta t)+L\,\delta t=p\,\delta q-H\,\delta t .\]

It follows that \(p=\partial_q S\) and \(-H=\partial_t S\). Eliminate \(p\) to obtain

\[\partial_t S+H(q,\partial_q S,t)=0 .\]

The equation involves partial derivatives because it describes an entire family of possible endpoints. By contrast, \(dS/dt=L\) along a single trajectory. A useful dimensional check is that a derivative of action with respect to length is momentum, whereas a derivative with respect to time is energy.

Separate time and build a clock from energy

For a time-independent Hamiltonian try \(S=W(q;E)-Et\). With \(H=p^2/(2m)+U(q)\), this gives \((\partial_qW)^2=2m(E-U)\). A trajectory on the increasing-position branch has positive momentum, so an integral from a fixed reference position \(q_0\) supplies \(W\). Differentiate at fixed final position with respect to the family parameter \(E\):

\[W=\int_{q_0}^{q}\!\sqrt{2m(E-U(s))}\,ds,\]
\[\partial_EW=\int_{q_0}^{q}\!\frac{m\,ds}{\sqrt{2m(E-U(s))}} .\]

The integrand of the second integral is \(1/v\). Hence \(\partial_E W\) is the travel time from the reference point. Setting \(\partial_E S=0\) chooses a departure time of zero with this lower integration limit. A different constant amounts to shifting the clock. At a turning point \(p=0\), the one-way branch must be changed to continue the motion.

Work two arrival-time problems

Let \(q=x\) point downwards, \(U=-mgx\), and release the bead through \(x=0\) with speed \(u\). Its energy is \(E=mu^2/2\). The integral yields \(t=(\sqrt{u^2+2gx}-u)/g\). Squaring the relation after isolating the square root gives \(x=ut+gt^2/2\), the same trajectory as Newton's equation. For \(u=2.0\) m/s, \(g=9.81\) m/s² and \(x=3.0\) m the arrival time is about \(0.604\) s. The endpoint momentum uses the final speed, about \(7.93\) m/s, rather than the average over the fall.

Reverse the experiment by pointing \(x\) upward in the same field. If \(u=4.0\) m/s, the maximum height is \(0.815\) m. A gate \(0.50\) m above launch is crossed twice. From \(x=ut-gt^2/2\) the two times are \(0.154\) s and \(0.661\) s. The need for two branches of \(W\) is experimentally visible in those two timestamps. A calculation that takes the positive square root everywhere loses the return journey.

Compare two launch speeds

The plot shows upward trajectories and their common observation height. Lower the launch speed towards the value that barely reaches the gate. Watch what happens to the separation between the two arrival times.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on the double root

A gate records two crossings 0.40 s apart in a uniform field of 9.81 m/s². How high does the trajectory rise above that gate?

Work through the solution

The two crossings are symmetric about the maximum. The fall from the maximum takes 0.20 s, giving \(g(0.20)^2/2=0.1962\) m. Equivalently the speed at the gate is \(g(0.40)/2=1.962\) m/s and its kinetic energy per unit mass gives the same height.

Estimate the arrival-time sensitivity of a gate on the rising branch. At \(u=4.0\,\mathrm{m/s}\) compare \(h=0.50\) m with \(h=0.80\) m.

Work through the solution

Implicit differentiation of \(h=ut-gt^2/2\) at fixed launch speed gives \(dt/dh=1/(u-gt)\). On ascent this is \(1/\sqrt{u^2-2gh}\). Its value grows from \(0.402\,\mathrm{s/m}\) at 0.50 m to \(1.81\,\mathrm{s/m}\) at 0.80 m. Close to the top a small height error produces much larger timing uncertainty because the trajectory crosses slowly.

Does adding a constant \(C\) to a potential alter the predicted arrival times from Hamilton–Jacobi theory when the initial position and speed are unchanged?

Work through the solution

Both the total energy label and potential shift by \(C\). Hence \(p=\sqrt{2m(E-U)}\) and the abbreviated action as a path integral of \(p\) are unchanged for that orbit. The full action acquires \(-Ct\). Its spatial derivative and the time crossing predictions remain the same. Comparing the same numerical energy before and after the shift would specify different trajectories.

Further reading · Fließbach, Hamiltonformalismus · Tong, Hamilton–Jacobi theory.

Chapter 15 · Read a period from the area enclosed by an orbit

A track with a V-shaped potential has straight force segments but a strongly amplitude-dependent period. Its area in phase space gives a useful way to predict both the period and a slow change of the track.

Construct the closed area

For one bound degree of freedom at energy \(E\), position runs between two turning points. In a mechanical Hamiltonian \(p^2/(2m)+U(q)\), the upper branch of the phase portrait has positive momentum and the lower branch has its negative. The directed integral around one full orbit counts the enclosed area. Define the action \(J\) using the area divided by \(2\pi\). This choice makes its conjugate phase an angle measured in radians:

\[J(E)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint p\,dq=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{q_-}^{q_+}\sqrt{2m(E-U(q))}\,dq .\]

Now change the energy slightly. The integrand vanishes at each regular endpoint, so the moving-endpoint contributions to the derivative vanish. In the interior \(\partial p/\partial E=m/p=1/v\). The derivative of the upper-branch area therefore measures the time to reach the opposite turning point.

\[\frac{dJ}{dE}=\frac{T(E)}{2\pi},\]
\[\omega(E)=\frac{dE}{dJ}.\]

This argument also explains why the construction becomes delicate near a separatrix. A trajectory can spend an arbitrarily long time near an unstable equilibrium, and \(T\) can diverge even though the enclosed area stays finite.

Carry out the integral for a constant restoring force

Use \(U(q)=F|q|\) with \(F>0\). The turning amplitude is \(A=E/F\). Integrate the positive branch from \(0\) to \(A\) and use reflection symmetry. Substitution \(s=E-Fq\) gives

\[J=\frac{4\sqrt{2m}}{3\pi F}E^{3/2},\]
\[T=\frac{4\sqrt{2mE}}{F}=4\sqrt{\frac{2mA}{F}} .\]

The answer has an independent mechanical check. From a turning point the bead accelerates uniformly with magnitude \(F/m\) until the centre. Covering a distance \(A\) from rest takes \(\sqrt{2mA/F}\), a quarter period. Doubling the amplitude increases the full period by \(\sqrt{2}\). The abrupt change of force direction at the centre leaves the velocity continuous.

Slowly steepen the track

A slowly adjusted parameter can preserve the orbit action to leading order when the motion remains periodic and many cycles fit inside the adjustment time. For this trap \(m\) stays constant, so constant \(J\) requires \(E^{3/2}/F\) to stay fixed. Therefore \(E\propto F^{2/3}\), \(A=E/F\propto F^{-1/3}\) and \(T\propto F^{-2/3}\). Work done by adjusting the slope changes the energy despite the approximate conservation of action.

Start with \(m=0.25\) kg, \(F=2.0\) N and \(A=0.080\) m. The initial energy is \(0.160\) J and the period \(0.566\) s. A slow eightfold increase of \(F\) makes the energy four times larger, \(0.640\) J, and the amplitude half as large, \(0.040\) m. The period becomes \(0.141\) s. In contrast, switching slopes instantaneously at the centre leaves the kinetic energy and position unchanged at that instant. The same final slope would then give amplitude \(0.010\) m. The route by which a parameter changes matters.

Compare closed orbits at constant action

The axes are position and momentum. Change the slope ratio. Predict how the turning positions and the central momentum move in opposite directions. Their changes balance in the enclosed area.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on inferring a change

After a slow adjustment the period is 64% of its original value. Find the force and amplitude ratios.

Work through the solution

From \(T_f/T_i=(F_f/F_i)^{-2/3}\), the force ratio is \(0.64^{-3/2}=1.953125\). The amplitude ratio is its inverse cube root, \(0.800\). Squaring the period formula with these values provides an independent check.

Derive a relation between the area action and the product of energy and period in the V trap. What fraction of the total energy is kinetic on a time average over one orbit?

Work through the solution

The explicit formulas give \(J=ET/(3\pi)\), a quantity with units of action. Along the orbit \(p\,dq=p\dot q\,dt=2K\,dt\). Hence \(2\pi J=2T\langle K\rangle\) and \(\langle K\rangle=E/3\), leaving \(2E/3\) in potential energy. This is an independent check using a measurable time average.

A trap is weakened gradually until the amplitude doubles. Find the predicted changes in energy and period, and state when the slow-ramp argument is most vulnerable.

Work through the solution

The amplitude varies as \(F^{-1/3}\), so doubling it requires reducing \(F\) to one eighth. The energy then falls to one quarter and the period increases fourfold. A ramp duration adequate initially can become too short compared to the final period. Checking the endpoint period alone is insufficient if the control has sharp changes during the ramp.

Further reading · Tong, action–angle coordinates and adiabatic invariants.

Chapter 16 · From a chain of springs to an elastic field

A rod ringing after a small axial displacement can be described by one differential equation at every point. Derive that field equation, its boundary conditions and the range of wavelengths for which it represents a discrete chain.

Take a limit that represents the same rod

Divide a rod of cross-sectional area \(A\), mass density \(\rho\), length \(L\) and Young modulus \(Y\) into segments of length \(a\). Let \(u_j(t)\) be the axial displacement at a site. Each segment carries mass \(\rho Aa\). Under a small relative displacement \(\Delta u\) the strain is \(\Delta u/a\). Hooke's law gives force \(YA\Delta u/a\), hence the segment spring constant is \(YA/a\). Refining the chain must change both its masses and its springs by these rules to preserve the material.

\[\rho Aa\,\ddot u_j=\frac{YA}{a}(u_{j+1}-2u_j+u_{j-1}).\]

For a smooth displacement the second difference is \(a^2u_{xx}+a^4u_{xxxx}/12+\cdots\). Cancel a common factor of \(Aa\). The leading continuum equation is \(\rho u_{tt}=Yu_{xx}\) with wave speed \(c=\sqrt{Y/\rho}\). The next term already shows why sharp disturbances with wavelengths close to \(a\) require the discrete chain.

Vary the field and keep the endpoint term

Alternatively add the kinetic and elastic energy densities using \(u_x\) for strain. The action per unit length integrated over time is

\[S=\int dt\int_0^L dx\,\left(\frac{\rho A}{2}u_t^2-\frac{YA}{2}u_x^2\right).\]

Vary \(u\) by any smooth small displacement that vanishes at the initial and final times. Integrating its time derivative by parts returns \(-\rho A u_{tt}\). Integrating the space derivative by parts returns \(+\partial_x(YA u_x)\) together with an endpoint term \(-[YA u_x\,\delta u]_0^L\). The interior coefficient must vanish independently. A clamped end fixes \(\delta u=0\) there. A free end permits arbitrary virtual displacement and thus requires zero axial traction \(YA u_x=0\). An externally applied force would contribute boundary work to the action. This reasoning keeps the correct equation even when stiffness and cross section vary with position.

Measure the first resonance

For a uniform rod clamped at zero, \(u=X(x)\cos(\omega t)\) gives \(X=C\sin(kx)\), with \(\omega=ck\). A free end at \(L\) requires \(\cos(kL)=0\), so \(k_n=(n+1/2)\pi/L\). With \(L=0.80\) m, \(Y=70.0\) GPa and \(\rho=2700\) kg/m³, \(c=5092\) m/s and the first axial resonance is \(c/(4L)=1591\) Hz. Clamping both ends requires \(X(L)=0\), changing that first frequency to 3183 Hz. The material has stayed the same while the measured spectrum has changed.

For the infinite chain substitute \(u_j=e^{i(kja-\omega t)}\) directly into the second difference. The factors sum to \(2\cos(ka)-2=-4\sin^2(ka/2)\). Hence \(\omega=2c|\sin(ka/2)|/a\). At \(ka=\pi/2\) the ratio to the continuum frequency is \(2\sqrt2/\pi=0.9003\). Shorter wavelength changes the phase advance between neighbouring sites more strongly, making the smooth-gradient approximation less accurate.

Test wavelength resolution

Change the number of sites per wavelength and read the discrete frequency relative to the continuum prediction. Compare the phase speed with the group speed \(d\omega/dk\), which controls a narrow packet of modes.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on the continuum error

A mesh has ten sites per wavelength. Estimate its fractional phase-speed error first by series expansion, then with the sine formula.

Work through the solution

With \(x=ka/2=\pi/10\), the ratio is \(\sin x/x\). Keeping \(1-x^2/6\) predicts a deficit of 0.01645. Direct evaluation gives 0.01637, about 1.64%. Doubling the sites per wavelength reduces the leading error by a factor of four.

A frequency scan of the same rod finds equally spaced resonances from 1.59 kHz onwards at 3.18 kHz intervals. Predict the axial displacement at the support and the stress at the opposite end. Which observation distinguishes a clamped–free from a free–free rod?

Work through the solution

The sequence \(f_n=(2n+1)c/(4L)\) agrees with the clamped–free case. At the clamp displacement vanishes, while at the opposite end the axial derivative and therefore stress vanish. A free–free rod also has zero stress at both ends, but its nonzero resonances are integer multiples of \(c/(2L)\); it also permits a zero-frequency rigid translation.

For a narrow wave packet at four sites per central wavelength, use the dispersion relation to compare the arrival of the centre of the packet with a single crest.

Work through the solution

Here \(ka/2=\pi/4\). The phase travels at \(c\sin(\pi/4)/(\pi/4)=0.9003c\), whereas the envelope moves at \(c\cos(\pi/4)=0.7071c\). Over a fixed distance the packet centre arrives about \(0.9003/0.7071=1.27\) times as late as a crest with that phase speed. A broadband pulse need not have one well-defined group speed.

Further reading · Fließbach, Kontinuumsmechanik · Tong, Phonons.

Chapter 17 · Proper time, momentum and a moving clock

A pair of light pulses lets observers compare clocks without assuming a universal time. Build the invariant interval and use it to calculate transit times and the energy exchanged by moving material.

Identify what every inertial observer can agree on

Assign coordinates \(x,t\) to events using rulers and synchronized clocks in an inertial laboratory. A light ray moving along the positive or negative axis obeys \(x=\pm ct\). A linear transformation between two inertial laboratories must take each family of light rays into itself if both measure the same \(c\). Introduce \(u=ct-x\) and \(w=ct+x\), coordinates constant on the two families. A boost rescales them. Reciprocity between inertial frames and spatial reflection give opposite rescalings, \(u'=e^\eta u,\ w'=e^{-\eta}w\). Here the additive parameter \(\eta\) is called rapidity.

\[c^2t'^2-x'^2=u'w'=uw=c^2t^2-x^2.\]

Adding and subtracting the transformed coordinates gives \(ct'=ct\cosh\eta-x\sinh\eta\) and \(x'=x\cosh\eta-ct\sinh\eta\). The moving origin obeys \(x'=0\), so \(v/c=\tanh\eta\) and \(\cosh\eta=\gamma=1/\sqrt{1-v^2/c^2}\). These statements concern pairs of events. An experiment involving a rod must separately specify which endpoint events are simultaneous in the observer's frame.

Follow one clock along its trajectory

Events on the same material clock have \(dx'=0\) instantaneously in its rest frame. The invariant interval then gives \(d\tau=dt\sqrt{1-v(t)^2/c^2}\). Over a curved path integrate this local relation. Two synchronized clocks reunited at the same event can have accumulated different proper times because their intervening paths differ.

Consider an inertially moving clock passing two fixed laboratory gates 12 light-microseconds apart. With \(v=0.80c\) the lab crossing interval is 15 µs. The travelling clock accumulates \(15/1.6667=9.0\) µs. It measures the simultaneous distance between gates in its own frame as 7.2 light-microseconds. Dividing that distance by \(0.80c\) gives the same 9.0 µs. Combining a laboratory distance with a clock time from another frame would obscure this check.

From a clock to energy and momentum

Differentiate the spacetime position with respect to the invariant proper time. The resulting four-velocity transforms as a four-vector. For a particle of constant rest mass \(m\), multiply by that mass to obtain the four-momentum \(p^\mu=(E/c,\mathbf p)=m\gamma(c,\mathbf v)\). The invariant norm is obtained in the rest frame and gives

\[E^2-p^2c^2=m^2c^4,\]
\[K=E-mc^2 .\]

For low speed, expanding \(\gamma\) returns \(K=mv^2/2\) to lowest order. For light, the massless relation \(E=pc\) holds without defining a rest frame. These elementary cases are useful checks when adding the four-momenta of a system.

Imagine two identical material samples with equal and opposite speeds \(0.60c\) close into a sealed calorimeter at rest in their centre-of-momentum frame. Ignoring the original container mass and any energy escaping, the total momentum vanishes and the invariant mass of the combined contents is \(2\gamma m=2.50m\). The excess \(0.50mc^2\) over the original rest energies is internal energy in this frame. Energy conservation applies throughout the process.

Vary the gate experiment

Increase speed at fixed laboratory gate separation. Predict both the coordinate time and the travelling clock's proper time. At a fixed speed, doubling the distance doubles both readings.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on identifying the frame

A laboratory separates two gates by 24 light-microseconds. A travelling clock measures 18 µs between crossings. Determine the speed.

Work through the solution

In units with distance in light-microseconds, the lab time is \(24/\beta\) µs. The interval gives \(18^2=(24/\beta)^2-24^2\), so \(\beta=24/30=0.800\). The laboratory time is 30 µs. This inversion uses one distance and two events without assuming the clocks display the same elapsed time.

An emission and reception have laboratory separation \(6\) light-microseconds and time difference \(10\,\mu\mathrm s\). Could one inertial clock record both, and which speed would it need?

Work through the solution

The interval is timelike because \(10^2-6^2=64>0\). The unique inertial path between the two events has speed \(0.6c\). Its measured time is \(8\,\mu\mathrm s\), consistent with \(10/\gamma\) and \(\gamma=1.25\). A laboratory separation of 12 light-microseconds at the same time difference would be spacelike and could not be joined by such a clock.

In the laboratory a single light pulse has energy \(E\) and momentum \(E/c\). Compare its invariant mass with that of two pulses of energy \(E\) travelling in opposite directions together as a system.

Work through the solution

For one pulse \(E^2-p^2c^2=0\). For the pair the energies add to \(2E\) and the vector momenta cancel. The system therefore has invariant mass \(2E/c^2\). The invariant mass of a composite system depends on total four-momentum. Individual invariant masses alone do not specify it.

Further reading · Fließbach, Relativistische Mechanik · Tong, Dynamics and Relativity, chapter 7.

Chapter 18 · Liouville flow and what an ensemble can remember

A cloud of identical noninteracting beads is released with slightly different initial positions and momenta. Its position distribution widens. Does the system occupy more phase space, or has its uncertainty merely changed shape?

Follow a small phase-space element

For one canonical degree of freedom the velocity of a point \((q,p)\) is \((\partial_pH,-\partial_qH)\). A short-time map takes \(\boldsymbol z\) to \(\boldsymbol z+\boldsymbol v(\boldsymbol z,t)\,dt\). Its Jacobian matrix is \(I+(\partial\boldsymbol v/\partial\boldsymbol z)dt\); expanding the determinant gives \(1+(\nabla_z\cdot\boldsymbol v)dt+O(dt^2)\). Hamilton's equations make the divergence zero because the two mixed second derivatives of a smooth Hamiltonian cancel. Each additional canonical pair contributes a similar cancellation. A time-dependent Hamiltonian may change energy but still preserve the instantaneous phase-space volume along the flow.

Conservation of the number of ensemble members in a moving phase-space box also gives the continuity equation \(\partial_t f+\nabla_z\cdot(f\boldsymbol v)=0\). The vanishing divergence leaves \(df/dt=0\) on each trajectory. A stationary mechanical system can admit \(f=F(H)\) as a stationary distribution because trajectories stay on energy surfaces. Such stationarity alone does not imply that one trajectory explores an entire energy surface. For example, further integrals of motion restrict regular multidimensional trajectories.

Propagate a cloud released into free flight

For mass \(m\), the exact map is \(p=p_0,\ q=q_0+tp_0/m\). Its determinant is one regardless of time. An initially uncorrelated cloud with variances \(s_q^2,s_p^2\) has evolved covariance \(C_{qp}=ts_p^2/m\). The new position variance is \(s_q^2+(ts_p/m)^2\), while the momentum variance stays fixed. Compute the determinant of this covariance matrix:

\[\sigma_q^2\sigma_p^2-C_{qp}^2=s_q^2s_p^2 .\]

For a Gaussian the ellipse at a fixed multiple of the covariance radius thus retains its area while it shears. With \(m=1\,\mathrm{kg}\), \(s_q=1\,\mathrm m\), \(s_p=0.5\,\mathrm{kg\,m/s}\), and \(t=4\,\mathrm s\), the observed position standard deviation is \(\sqrt5=2.236\,\mathrm m\). The momentum standard deviation remains 0.5 and the covariance becomes \(1.0\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s}\). Their uncorrected width product is 1.118 in action units. The square root of the covariance determinant is still 0.5. Multiplication of measured marginal widths alone falsely suggests an area increase.

Compare reversible shear and real drag

Now apply a linear drag \(\dot p=-\gamma p\) to each bead with constant \(\gamma>0\). Its phase-space divergence is \(-\gamma\), hence the map determinant after time \(t\) is \(e^{-\gamma t}\). Direct integration gives \(p=e^{-\gamma t}p_0,\ q=q_0+(1-e^{-\gamma t})p_0/(m\gamma)\). The cloud now genuinely loses area in the reduced bead description. A larger Hamiltonian model of bead plus environment can remain volume preserving. Choose a drag rate in the graph and compare the original, freely sheared and damped ellipses for the same observation time. Check there is a smooth zero-drag limit rather than substituting zero into a quotient.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on distinguishing a shear from dissipation

A harmonic oscillator maps its scaled quadratures by a rotation. Show why an initially anisotropic cloud can periodically sharpen in position without phase-space cooling.

Work through the solution

Write its covariance as \(R\Sigma_0R^T\). Since \(\det R=1\), its determinant is unchanged. A rotation of one quarter turn exchanges the original widths, so position can sharpen when the original momentum width in scaled units was smaller. Energy extraction is unnecessary.

At \(t=3\,\mathrm s\) the measured ellipse area is 55 percent of the initial area in the linear-drag model. Infer \(\gamma\). Which independent observable checks the inference?

Work through the solution

\(\gamma=-\ln(0.55)/3=0.199\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}\). The ratio of final to initial momentum standard deviations should also be 0.55 for drag without random forcing. Added environmental fluctuations would modify that prediction.

Further reading · Cambridge classical dynamics, Hamiltonian flow and Liouville's theorem.

Chapter 19 · Parametric resonance and Floquet growth

A pendulum can gain energy when its length varies periodically even if no additive force acts on it. A modulation near twice a natural frequency amplifies one phase of the oscillation and attenuates another. The stability test concerns the growth of a perturbation over a complete modulation period.

Linearise the timing mechanism

Consider \(\ddot x+2\gamma\dot x+\omega_0^2[1+h\cos(\Omega t)]x=0\). Here \(h\) is the fractional modulation of the stiffness and \(\gamma\) is the amplitude damping rate. If \(x=\dot x=0\) exactly, the solution stays zero. Small fluctuations or an initial condition provide the seed. Direct differentiation of \(E=[\dot x^2+\omega_0^2(1+h\cos\Omega t)x^2]/2\) yields \(\dot E=-2\gamma\dot x^2-h\omega_0^2\Omega x^2\sin(\Omega t)/2\). Energy transfer depends on the phase of \(x^2\), which naturally contains a component at twice the oscillation frequency.

Near \(\nu=\Omega/2\simeq\omega_0\), write \(x=A(t)e^{i\nu t}+A^*(t)e^{-i\nu t}\) with slowly varying \(A\). Multiplication by \(\cos(2\nu t)\) mixes the negative-frequency term back into the positive-frequency term with coefficient \(h\omega_0^2/2\). There is also a component at \(3\nu\), rapidly oscillating in this first-resonance approximation. Retain terms proportional to \(e^{i\nu t}\), neglect \(\ddot A\) and products of small damping with \(\dot A\). This gives \(2i\nu\dot A+(\omega_0^2-\nu^2+2i\gamma\nu)A+(h\omega_0^2/2)A^*=0\).

Find the first instability tongue

Set \(\Delta=\nu-\omega_0\) and keep leading terms in the small ratios \(h,\gamma/\omega_0,|\Delta|/\omega_0\). The slow equations are \(\dot A=(-\gamma-i\Delta)A+igA^*\) and their conjugate, where \(g=h\omega_0/4\). The two-by-two characteristic equation is \((s+\gamma)^2=g^2-\Delta^2\). The faster exponent is

\[\operatorname{Re}s_+=-\gamma+\operatorname{Re}\sqrt{(h\omega_0/4)^2-\Delta^2}.\]

The oscillation grows when \(g^2>\gamma^2+\Delta^2\). Exactly on resonance this gives \(h>4\gamma/\omega_0\). With \(\omega_0=10\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\), \(\gamma=0.10\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\) and \(h=0.08\), \(g=0.20\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\), giving an on-resonance doubling time \(\ln2/0.10=6.93\,\mathrm s\). The approximate growing interval in drive angular frequency is \(20\pm0.346\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\). A large-amplitude real oscillator can saturate as its natural frequency changes with amplitude, a mechanism absent from this linear equation.

Compute the period map without averaging

Integrate the original linear equation over one drive period from two independent initial states \((1,0)\) and \((0,1)\). Their final columns form a matrix \(M\), which maps every initial perturbation over the same period \(T\). After \(j\) periods the map is \(M^j\); the maximum asymptotic amplitude exponent is \(\ln\max|\lambda_i(M)|/T\). Its determinant also has an exact check \(e^{-2\gamma T}\) from the divergence in the \((x,\dot x)\) plane. The calculation below integrates both columns by fourth-order Runge–Kutta. Compare the numerical Floquet exponent with the averaged prediction as the modulation depth increases. Both curves describe growth rates, including negative rates for decay.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on phase-sensitive amplification

At exact resonance and zero loss, substitute \(A=X+iY\) into \(\dot A=igA^*\). Identify the two special quadrature directions.

Work through the solution

Equating real and imaginary parts yields \(\dot X=gY,\ \dot Y=gX\). Along \(Y=X\) both grow at \(g\), whereas along \(Y=-X\) they decay at \(g\). These directions reflect how timing the stiffness change can add or remove work on each cycle.

On resonance an oscillator with \(\omega_0=40\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\) first grows near \(h=0.03\). Estimate its small-signal damping and its free amplitude e-folding time.

Work through the solution

From the weak-modulation threshold \(\gamma=h\omega_0/4=0.30\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\). Without modulation the underdamped free amplitude falls as \(e^{-\gamma t}\), giving \(3.33\,\mathrm s\). Threshold uncertainty or nonlinear frequency shifts propagate into this inference.

Further reading · Verhulst, Utrecht University, perturbation analysis of parametric resonance.

Problem sheet 7 · Reconstructing an accelerating trap

An optical stage can produce either a harmonic potential or a triangular one. Use a harmonic trap to calibrate the stage acceleration, then predict the motion in a different trap whose orbit area can be integrated analytically. Record each prediction before changing a control.

Calibrate the acceleration from the trace

A \(0.50\) kg test carriage in a spring of stiffness \(8.0\,\mathrm{N/m}\) starts at rest relative to the support. After a sudden change to constant support acceleration, its first relative turning point lies at \(-0.20\,\mathrm m\) at \(0.785\,\mathrm s\). Infer the acceleration using the signed displacement. Use the time independently to check the assumed stiffness.

Work through the solution

In the support frame the equilibrium is \(Q_*=-ma/k\), and a carriage starting at the old centre reaches \(2Q_*\) at its first turning point. Thus \(Q_*=-0.10\,\mathrm m\) and \(a=1.60\,\mathrm{m/s^2}\). The half-period is \(\pi\sqrt{m/k}=0.7854\,\mathrm s\). The time tests \(k/m\) without requiring the displacement calibration.

Reproduce the displacement and turning time in the accelerating harmonic trap

Check an independent calibration with a slow change of slope

Return the support to inertial motion and fit a \(0.25\) kg test mass in \(U=F|Q|\). Begin at \(F=2.0\) N, amplitude \(0.080\) m. Increase the slope slowly by a factor of eight. Predict the final energy, turning amplitude and period. Which combination of measured turning amplitude and slope would test preservation of orbit area?

Work through the solution

The orbit action is proportional to \(E^{3/2}/F\) at fixed mass. Hence \(E_f=0.160\times8^{2/3}=0.640\,\mathrm J\), \(A_f=E_f/16=0.040\,\mathrm m\), and \(T_f=4\sqrt{2mA_f/F_f}=0.1414\,\mathrm s\). Substitution of \(E=FA\) gives \(J^2\propto FA^3\), so the measured product \(FA^3\) should stay fixed in the slow limit. Check this product as well as the period.

Compare the two measured phase-space areas

Combine the two experiments

Restore the initial triangular slope. Ramp the calibrated stage acceleration \(a=1.60\,\mathrm{m/s^2}\) from zero over many periods, smoothly at each endpoint. Derive the new right and left turning distances relative to the support. Predict whether the orbit area changes at leading order.

Work through the solution

The inertial contribution to potential energy is \(maQ\). The two positive slope magnitudes are \(F+ma\) on the right and \(F-ma\) on the left. Both must remain positive for confinement. Integrating each half of the triangular orbit separately gives \(J\propto E^{3/2}[1/(F+ma)+1/(F-ma)]\). Set this equal to the original value \(2E_0^{3/2}/F\). Writing \(s=ma/F=0.20\) gives \(E=E_0(1-s^2)^{2/3}=0.1557\,\mathrm J\). The right distance is \(E/2.4=0.0649\,\mathrm m\), and the left distance \(E/1.6=0.0973\,\mathrm m\). The orbit becomes asymmetric while its area is conserved to leading adiabatic order.

Design a falsification test

If both turning positions have a common, unknown camera magnification, which quantity still tests the calibrated acceleration? What limit makes the assumption of a slowly adjusted bound orbit fail?

Work through the solution

Divide the left distance by the right. The common energy and camera scale cancel, leaving \((F+ma)/(F-ma)=1.50\). As \(|ma|\) approaches \(F\), one confining slope approaches zero and the period grows. A ramp slow at zero acceleration may then fail the separation-of-time-scales condition. At \(|ma|\ge F\) the potential no longer confines both sides.

Problem sheet 8 · Can the intermediate axis instability be measured before a body turns over?

A marked rigid body rotates in free flight. A camera sees an apparently steady rotation followed by a rapid change of orientation. Obtain a growth rate from torque balance, identify the invariant deciding the local geometry and design a measurement that separates exponential growth from a bad estimate of the initial alignment.

Obtain the equations in the frame that moves with the body

Let \(I_1<I_2<I_3\) be distinct principal moments. Express angular momentum in body components. If there is no applied torque, explain why the time derivatives of those three components do not all vanish. Derive the equation for each angular velocity before considering any equilibrium.

Work through the solution

A body basis vector has inertial derivative \(\boldsymbol{\omega}\times\mathbf e_i\). Differentiating the expansion of angular momentum in that basis gives \((d\mathbf L/dt)_{\rm inertial}=(d\mathbf L/dt)_{\rm body}+\boldsymbol{\omega}\times\mathbf L\). In principal axes the inertia tensor is constant and diagonal, so \(L_i=I_i\omega_i\). Setting the left hand side to zero gives

\[I_1\dot\omega_1=(I_2-I_3)\omega_2\omega_3,\]
\[I_2\dot\omega_2=(I_3-I_1)\omega_3\omega_1,\]
\[I_3\dot\omega_3=(I_1-I_2)\omega_1\omega_2.\]

Multiply the equations by their respective \(\omega_i\) and add. The coefficients sum to zero and the kinetic energy is constant. Dot the vector equation with \(\mathbf L\) and the cross product drops out, proving conservation of its norm. These are two independent checks on a numerical trace.

Identify the instability from two conserved quantities

Set the nominal spin to \(\omega_2=\Omega\). Linearise in the two transverse components and find the characteristic exponents. Then explain the same phenomenon without differentiating a perturbation a second time.

Work through the solution

The equation for \(\dot\omega_2\) contains only the product of the small perturbations. To first order \(\Omega\) stays fixed. Put \(a=(I_3-I_2)\Omega/I_1\), \(b=(I_2-I_1)\Omega/I_3\). The remaining equations are \(\dot\omega_1=-a\omega_3\) and \(\dot\omega_3=-b\omega_1\). Both \(a\) and \(b\) are positive for positive spin, so \(\ddot\omega_1=ab\omega_1\). The eigenvalues are \(\pm\sqrt{ab}\). Changing the sense of rotation exchanges the directions that grow and decay.

At fixed angular momentum magnitude, \(\mathbf L\) lies on a sphere. Eliminate \(L_2^2\) from the energy using that sphere. The resulting difference from steady intermediate-axis energy is exact.

\[2T-|\mathbf L|^2/I_2=(1/I_1-1/I_2)L_1^2+(1/I_3-1/I_2)L_3^2.\]

One coefficient is positive and the other negative. Energy has a saddle at the intermediate orientation. At the minimum or maximum inertia axis those two coefficients have the same sign. The nearby energy contours close around those two spins. Conservation of energy alone does not select stability until one states which other invariant restricts the motion.

Read a doubling time without knowing the original tilt

Take principal moments \(0.030,0.040,0.050\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}\), spin \(12\,\mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}\), and \(\omega_1(0)=0.10\,\mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}\), \(\omega_3(0)=0\). Estimate when the first component reaches 0.20. Compare with a fit that assumes an exponential from the first frame.

Work through the solution

The rates are \(a=4.0\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\), \(b=2.4\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\) and \(\sigma=\sqrt{9.6}=3.0984\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\). The zero initial slope places equal weight on the increasing and decreasing solutions. Thus \(\omega_1(t)=0.10\cosh(\sigma t)\). It first doubles at \(\operatorname{arcosh}(2)/\sigma=0.425\,\mathrm{s}\). A pure growing mode doubles in \(\ln(2)/\sigma=0.224\,\mathrm{s}\). An initially horizontal tangent is compatible with linear instability.

To excite the growing mode alone, prepare \(\omega_3(0)=-\sigma\omega_1(0)/a\). The relevant marker here reads the body components of angular velocity. Projecting a fixed line of sight onto a body mark introduces a separate kinematic modulation. A vector gyroscope or a reconstruction using multiple marks separates orientation from angular velocity.

Compare a perturbation calculation with all three Euler equations

The graph integrates the full equations with the initial transverse velocity along the first axis. Predict which portion agrees with a hyperbolic cosine. Vary the initial perturbation and spin independently. At the same time compare two initially distinct motions that both stay near the middle axis for several frames.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Work through the solution

For small initial amplitude the neglected correction to \(\dot\omega_2\) is quadratic. A smaller seed leaves a longer interval before the linear approximation loses accuracy. Doubling the spin doubles the local growth rate. A perfectly aligned spin is an exact solution with an unstable neighbourhood. Numerical or experimental perturbations need a finite time to grow. Once a transverse component approaches the nominal spin, the hyperbolic cosine can grow without bound while the true components are restricted by the angular momentum sphere. That separation diagnoses a depleted range of validity even in an accurate integration.

For a second treatment of rigid-body motion and the body frame, work through the rigid bodies chapter and second problem sheet in David Tong’s Cambridge course.

Forces, constraints and a real instrument

Use virtual work to choose generalised coordinates. Test the predictions for a moving support, a constrained slider and an instrument that exchanges mechanical and electrical energy.

  3. Finding a displacement when no load acts in its direction
  4. Coordinates, energy and equivalent inertia
  5. Energy coordinates and Lagrange’s equation

  7. When a suspended capacitor loses its stable equilibrium
  8. A moving-coil galvanometer as an electrical and mechanical system

From cut forces to local equilibrium

Engineering mechanics

A support reaction tells you the total force entering a part. To predict what an inclined internal surface carries, keep track of both the orientation of the cut and the direction of the force across it.

A force that depends on the surface you choose

Imagine a small triangular wedge cut out of a thin, slowly loaded plate. Its sloping face has unit outward normal \(\mathbf n=(n_x,n_y)\) and area \(A\). The two faces normal to the coordinate axes have areas \(A n_x\) and \(A n_y\) when both components are positive. Name the internal force per unit area on a face its traction. Opposite faces of the same cut carry equal and opposite tractions. Gravity and acceleration act on volume, which shrinks faster than area as the wedge shrinks. Dividing its force balance by \(A\) therefore gives a relation that no longer contains its size.

\[\mathbf t(\mathbf n)=n_x\mathbf t(\mathbf e_x)+n_y\mathbf t(\mathbf e_y)=\boldsymbol\sigma\mathbf n .\]

The columns of the stress matrix are the two coordinate-face tractions. Their linear combination supplies the force on any inclined face. A small square additionally needs zero net moment. To leading order, the shear forces on opposite faces form two couples of opposite sign and equal lever arm. Their balance requires \(\sigma_{xy}=\sigma_{yx}\) for an ordinary material without internal body couples.

Why a gradient of stress carries a body force

Now use a tiny rectangular element of side lengths \(dx,dy\) and unit thickness. The right-face contribution to horizontal force is \(\sigma_{xx}(x+dx,y)dy\); the left face contributes its negative evaluated at \(x\). Their difference is \(\partial_x\sigma_{xx}\,dx\,dy\) to leading order. Resolve the upper and lower shear tractions in the same way. Add a prescribed horizontal force per volume \(b_x\) and divide by volume. Repeating in the vertical direction produces two independent equations.

\[\partial_x\sigma_{xx}+\partial_y\sigma_{xy}+b_x=0,\]
\[\partial_x\sigma_{xy}+\partial_y\sigma_{yy}+b_y=0 .\]

These equations say nothing yet about how much the material stretches. They can test an assumed stress field before the elastic modulus has even been measured. A candidate must also reproduce the applied boundary tractions. Satisfying the interior equations alone leaves many possible stresses.

Worked example · the grip on a diagonal bonding plane

At a measurement point let \(\sigma_{xx}=60,\sigma_{yy}=20,\sigma_{xy}=10\) MPa. The outward normal is \((0.6,0.8)\). Matrix multiplication gives \(\mathbf t=(44,22)\) MPa. Projection along the normal gives \(0.6(44)+0.8(22)=44.0\) MPa tension. Projection on the unit tangent \((-0.8,0.6)\) gives \(-22.0\) MPa. Reversing the tangent changes the sign of reported shear. Reversing the surface normal reverses the traction vector itself. For a uniform 100 mm² patch the force has components 4.40 and 2.20 kN. Using only normal stress would miss the tangential load that the adhesive must transmit.

Worked example · a specimen with a known stress gradient

Stand a bar upright, support it at its bottom and leave the top free. Use upward \(y\), length \(L\), constant density \(\rho\), and tension positive. The vertical equation reads \(d\sigma_{yy}/dy-\rho g=0\). Integrating from the free top gives \(\sigma_{yy}=\rho g(y-L)\). For a 3.0 m aluminium bar of density 2700 kg/m³ the base stress is −79.5 kPa. Its upward support traction is positive because the bottom face has downward outward normal. A uniform cross-sectional area of 200 mm² gives a base reaction of 15.9 N, equal to the weight. The local and global checks involve different quantities but must agree.

Find the angles where the shear traction passes through zero. Compare each with an extremum of the normal traction. Change the sign of the imposed xy shear, sketch the new angles before moving the slider, and check whether the two extreme normal stresses change.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial · test a candidate before using a material law

In a region with \(x,y\) in metres, propose \(\sigma_{xx}=ax,\ \sigma_{xy}=-ay,\ \sigma_{yy}=0\), where \(a=2.0\) MPa/m. Does the field need a body force? Find the traction at \(x=0.30,\ y=0.20\) m on a face normal to positive \(x\).

Check the balance and the boundary reading

The horizontal divergence is \(a-a=0\). The vertical divergence is zero because the shear has no \(x\) variation. No body force is needed. The face sees the first column of the matrix, \((0.60,-0.40)\) MPa. A traction-free exterior at that location would reject this field even though it satisfies both interior equations.

Further reading · A. F. Bower, Applied Mechanics of Solids, §2.3, internal forces and stress

How a continuum balance becomes a finite-element stiffness matrix

Engineering mechanics

A single bar element can predict an end displacement exactly and still miss the stress field inside. The difference follows directly from the balance it is asked to satisfy.

Turn a differential balance into work against a test displacement

Let a straight small-strain bar of length \(L\) have area \(A\), Young’s modulus \(E\), displacement \(u(x)\) and distributed axial load \(q(x)\) in N/m. Tensile axial resultant is \(N=EAu'\). Force balance on a slice gives \(N'+q=0\). Clamp the left end and prescribe a right-end force \(P\). Multiply the balance by any smooth virtual displacement \(v\) satisfying \(v(0)=0\), integrate along the bar, and integrate the derivative of \(N\) by parts.

\[\int_0^L EA\,u'v'\,dx=P\,v(L)+\int_0^L qv\,dx .\]

The boundary term at the clamp disappears because that displacement is prescribed. The unknown reaction can later be recovered by equilibrium. This formulation requires only first derivatives of trial displacements. Piecewise linear displacements are therefore admissible even though their slopes may jump at internal nodes.

Compute the stiffness rather than memorising it

On an element from \(x=0\) to \(h\), interpolate the two nodal displacements linearly: \(u=(1-x/h)u_1+(x/h)u_2\). Let the test displacement have the same two basis functions. A virtual change of the first or second node gives the corresponding row of the balance. Both derivatives are constant with opposite signs.

\[B=\begin{bmatrix}-1/h&1/h\end{bmatrix},\]
\[K_e=\int_0^h B^T EA B\,dx=\frac{EA}{h}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\-1&1\end{bmatrix}.\]

For a constant distributed load, each basis function integrates to \(h/2\), so each node receives equivalent load \(qh/2\). The two rows of stiffness sum to zero. A uniform translation stores no strain energy. At a shared node the neighbouring virtual-work contributions add because both elements refer to the same unknown displacement.

Worked example · compare a nodal value with the stress in a cell

Take a 1 m bar with \(EA=2.0\) MN, uniform \(q=1200\) N/m and free right end. Exact balance integrated from \(N(L)=0\) gives \(N=q(L-x)\). Integrating strain from the clamp produces \(u=q(Lx-x^2/2)/(EA)\). The free-end displacement is 0.300 mm. With a single element the free row of the discrete system has stiffness \(EA/L\) and load \(qL/2\), giving the same displacement. Yet its piecewise constant axial force is \(EAu(L)/L=600\) N. The exact force ranges from 1200 N at the clamp to zero at the free end. The numerical force represents the mean over the cell.

Worked example · see what doubling resolution changes

Divide the bar into two equal elements. The middle node receives 600 N from both halves together, while the free node receives 300 N. The right element carries 300 N. Equilibrium of the middle node then puts 900 N in the left element. Their extensions are \(900(0.5)/(2\times10^6)=0.225\) mm and 0.075 mm. Nodal displacements agree with the exact quadratic at both nodes. The largest one-sided stress-resultant error within either element is now 300 N instead of 600 N. Comparing only tip displacements would hide this improvement.

Double the number of cells until the force error is below 100 N for the baseline load. Inspect the values on both sides of an element boundary and explain why agreement in tip displacement alone could have ended this refinement study too early.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Compare the bar’s nodal displacement with a OneSimulation truss assembled from axial members. Run the area-doubling case and check the predicted inverse-area change in displacement.

Tutorial · find the support reaction from the discarded row

For the two-element calculation, recover the left reaction using the assembled loads and displacement. Then derive an expression for the maximum axial-force error with \(n\) equal elements. Why can two different mesh studies give two different conclusions?

Evaluate the clamp row and the local profile

The left internal nodal force is \(-900\) N. The left external distributed-load share is \(+300\) N. Equilibrium requires an additional reaction \(-1200\) N. On an element of width \(L/n\) the exact linearly varying force changes by \(qL/n\); its constant midpoint approximation misses an endpoint by \(qL/(2n)\). Tip displacement and maximum local force answer different engineering questions. Mesh convergence must therefore name its measured quantity.

Further reading · A. F. Bower, Applied Mechanics of Solids, §8.1, finite-element equations for elastic solids

Finding a displacement when no load acts in its direction

Engineering mechanics

A displacement gauge measures the lateral travel of a truss joint under a vertical load. A force balance determines the bar forces, but cannot directly give the sideways motion. Introduce a dummy load, differentiate the stored energy and recover the gauge reading with its sign.

Link an external force coordinate to stored strain energy

For a linear elastic pin-jointed truss, neglect support movement and temperature changes and assume small displacements. Bar i has constant axial force \(N_i\), length \(L_i\), modulus \(E_i\) and area \(A_i\). Integrating its force against its extension gives \(U_i=N_i^2L_i/(2E_iA_i)\). Superposition makes each force linear in the external loads. A small increment of load changes the equilibrium configuration. For a linear elastic conservative system the complementary energy equals U and its derivative with respect to an independent applied force gives displacement along that force.

\[U=\sum_i{N_i^2L_i\over2E_iA_i},\]
\[\delta_Q={\partial U\over\partial Q}=\sum_i {N_i n_i L_i\over E_iA_i},\]
\[n_i={\partial N_i\over\partial Q}.\]

The derivative \(n_i\) is the member force produced per unit trial load in the requested direction, with all other independent loads held fixed. Solve this trial equilibrium even if \(Q=0\) physically. Setting \(Q=0\) before differentiating erases its entire contribution. As a dimensional check, \(n_i\) is dimensionless when Q and \(N_i\) both use newtons, and the sum has dimensions of length.

Compute the two-bar flexibility matrix

A joint hangs from two overhead pins by straight bars slanting up-left and up-right, each at 45° to horizontal. Positive joint coordinates are rightward and downward. Let V be the applied downward force and H the rightward force; bars carry tension positive. Equilibrium gives \(N_L=(V+H)/\sqrt2,\ N_R=(V-H)/\sqrt2\). Define bar axial compliances \(c_i=L_i/(E_iA_i)\). Insert the forces into the energy and differentiate with respect to H and V independently:

\[\begin{bmatrix}\delta_H\\\delta_V\end{bmatrix}={1\over2}\begin{bmatrix}c_L+c_R&c_L-c_R\\c_L-c_R&c_L+c_R\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}H\\V\end{bmatrix}.\]

The cross coefficients are equal because the mixed derivatives of the stored energy commute. This is Maxwell–Betti reciprocity. A cross displacement can be negative even though both direct compliances and stored energy are positive. The eigenvalues of this particular flexibility matrix are \(c_L,c_R\), corresponding to the two independent bar-extension directions.

Predict a sideways gauge reading and reverse the test

Take \(c_L=0.0020,\ c_R=0.0040\) mm/N. At \(V=100\) N and \(H=0\), both members carry 70.71 N. The vertical displacement is \(0.300\) mm, and the horizontal displacement is −0.100 mm. The more compliant right bar stretches farther; the joint moves left and down to accommodate both bar extensions. Half the vertical load times its displacement gives 15 N mm of strain energy. Summing the two bar energies independently gives the same result.

Apply instead \(H=100\) N and \(V=0\), assuming the slender right bar remains stable under compression. The vertical reading is −0.100 mm and horizontal motion is +0.300 mm. Thus the lateral response to the vertical test predicts the vertical response to a lateral test of equal magnitude. Equalizing bar areas would remove the cross response if the lengths and moduli matched. In practice a pin with clearance or a cable that goes slack can break the assumed linear bilateral response.

Exchange the left and right compliances. Before looking at the new graph, mark which readout should reverse sign and which should remain equal. Then set the two values alike and identify the symmetry that removes cross-coupling.

Use the course truss to test the same flexibility scaling on a larger assembly. Double every member area at fixed loads and geometry, then compare the joint displacement with the separately calculated chord force.
